Signature Rule Shell: A Design for a General Expert-System Shell JAH-ESYSS-000001 · 1

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Rule Shell is a Signature-made design for a general-purpose expert-system shell, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design separates the rule base from the inference engine, so the same engine can run a medical rule base in the morning and a configuration rule base in the afternoon, and it gives the shell an explanation facility that can show the chain of rules behind any conclusion. The design is presented as a study of what a good shell would contain, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of expert-system shells from MYCIN in the 1970s through CLIPS in 1985 to the business-rules engines of the 2000s. The design takes the shell pattern those systems established, a rule base, an inference engine, a working memory, and an explanation facility, and asks what a clean, modern version would contain if it were drawn from scratch today. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Rule Shell is a Signature-made design for a general-purpose expert-system shell, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design separates the rule base from the inference engine, so the same engine can run a medical rule base in the morning and a configuration rule base in the afternoon, and it gives the shell an explanation facility that can show the chain of rules behind any conclusion. The design is presented as a study of what a good shell would contain, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature Rule Shell, a Signature-made design-study for a general-purpose expert-system shell, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the domain knowledge as rules, each with a left-hand side of conditions and a right-hand side of conclusions. Step 2: load the rules into the shell's rule base and the facts into its working memory. Step 3: let the inference engine match and fire the rules, forward or backward as the task requires. Step 4: report the conclusion with the chain of rules behind it, so the user can check every step. The demo runs a small worked example of the chaining step with illustrative design values: it takes five inference stages with rules fired of 10, 18, 14, 22, and 8, and reports the total fired, the mean per stage, and the busiest stage. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"total_fired_design": 72, "mean_per_stage_design": 14.4, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the shell contains a rule base, an inference engine, a working memory, and an explanation facility, and the worked example shows the chaining arithmetic. The five stages fire 10, 18, 14, 22, and 8 rules, for a total of 72 rules fired, a mean of 14.4 per stage, and a busiest stage of 22. The result that matters is the design judgment: a shell that separates knowledge from inference can serve many domains, and the separation is what makes the design worth studying, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: max_stage: 22, mean: 14.4, stages: 5, total_fired: 72.

When It Applies

The Signature Rule Shell design applies as a study for anyone building a rule-based system: it lists the parts a shell needs, shows how they fit together, and gives the arithmetic for checking the inference. It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply where a working shell is needed, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Limits

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The shell is a design, not a system, so it has never been run on a real rule base, and its performance is unknown. The design assumes the rules can be written, which is the knowledge-acquisition bottleneck, and it does not solve that problem. The explanation facility assumes the chains are short enough to read, and a large rule base would produce chains too long to understand. The design is a study of what a shell should contain, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such a shell exists or works.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of a chain. The five stages fire 10, 18, 14, 22, and 8 rules, which sum to 72, and 72 divided by 5 is 14.4. The busiest stage fires 22 rules and the quietest fires 8. These are illustrative design values, and their transparency is the point: a design whose arithmetic can be checked is a design that can be argued with, and a design that cannot be checked is only a picture. Formal statement: total fired = sum of rules fired per stage; mean per stage = total fired / number of stages; largest stage = max of rules fired per stage. The equation states the chaining check the demo performs: it adds the rules fired at each stage of the worked example, averages them per stage, and picks out the busiest stage.

Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of separation: knowledge kept apart from inference can be changed without changing the engine, and an engine kept apart from knowledge can serve many domains. The design follows the public evidence that shells outlive applications, because the engine is reused while the rule bases change, and it adds the explanation facility as a requirement rather than an option, because a shell without explanation cannot be trusted in high-stakes work. The design is a study, so its claims are design claims, and they are judged by whether the design is complete and coherent, not by whether a real system was measured. Scientific relation: An inference chain fires rules stage by stage; its size is the total number of rule firings and its shape is how those firings spread across the stages.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

Working demo program

import hashlib
chain = [('match', 10), ('select', 18), ('fire', 14), ('update', 22), ('halt', 8)]
def compute():
    fired = [f for _, f in chain]
    total_fired = sum(fired)
    stages = len(fired)
    max_stage = max(fired)
    mean = round(total_fired / stages, 4)
    out = {"total_fired": total_fired, "stages": stages, "max_stage": max_stage, "mean": mean}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class ProgJAHESYSS000001:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        chain = [('match', 10), ('select', 18), ('fire', 14), ('update', 22), ('halt', 8)]
        def compute():
            fired = [f for _, f in chain]
            total_fired = sum(fired)
            stages = len(fired)
            max_stage = max(fired)
            mean = round(total_fired / stages, 4)
            out = {"total_fired": total_fired, "stages": stages, "max_stage": max_stage, "mean": mean}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(ProgJAHESYSS000001().run())

Signature Certainty Engine: A Design for Combining Uncertain Evidence JAH-ESYSS-000002 · 2

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Certainty Engine is a Signature-made design for combining uncertain evidence, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design takes the certainty-factor idea from MYCIN, a number from minus one to one for how strongly evidence supports a conclusion, and asks how a modern engine would combine such numbers, report them, and show the user where they came from. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of certainty factors from MYCIN in the 1970s. The design takes the MYCIN combination rule, two positive factors combine as their sum minus their product, and adds the reporting the original lacked: every combined value is shown with the pair that produced it, the chain is shown in full, and the engine refuses to report a certainty above a stated ceiling when the evidence is weak. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Certainty Engine is a Signature-made design for combining uncertain evidence, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design takes the certainty-factor idea from MYCIN, a number from minus one to one for how strongly evidence supports a conclusion, and asks how a modern engine would combine such numbers, report them, and show the user where they came from. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature Certainty Engine, a Signature-made design-study for combining uncertain evidence, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: assign a certainty factor to each piece of evidence, from minus one to one. Step 2: when a rule fires, combine the rule's factor with the factor of its premises. Step 3: when several rules support the same conclusion, combine their factors with the combination rule. Step 4: report the conclusion with its final factor and the full chain of combinations. The demo runs a small worked example of the combination step with illustrative design values: it combines three pairs of certainty factors, 0.7 with 0.5, 0.6 with 0.8, and 0.8 with 0.6, and reports each combined value and their mean. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"mean_combined_design": 0.8967, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the engine combines factors by the stated rule, reports every step, and caps the result when the evidence is weak. The worked example shows the combination arithmetic: 0.7 and 0.5 combine to 0.85, 0.6 and 0.8 combine to 0.92, and 0.8 and 0.6 combine to 0.92, for a mean combined certainty of 0.8967. The result that matters is the design judgment: an engine that shows every combination can be audited, and an engine that hides them cannot, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: mean_combined: 0.8967, n: 3.

When It Applies

The Signature Certainty Engine design applies as a study for anyone building a system that must report its confidence: it shows how to combine, report, and cap certainty factors, and gives the arithmetic for checking the combinations. It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply where calibrated probabilities are required, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Limits

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The engine is a design, not a system, so its factors have never been calibrated against real judgments, and its ceiling is a design choice rather than a measured threshold. The factors are scores, not probabilities, so they cannot be combined with statistical evidence. The design assumes the experts who assign the factors agree, which real experts often do not. The design is a study of how an engine should combine and report uncertainty, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such an engine exists or works.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the combination rule. For 0.7 and 0.5 the sum is 1.2 and the product is 0.35, so the combined value is 0.85. For 0.6 and 0.8 the sum is 1.4 and the product is 0.48, so the combined value is 0.92. For 0.8 and 0.6 the sum is 1.4 and the product is 0.48, so the combined value is 0.92. The mean of 0.85, 0.92, and 0.92 is 2.69 divided by 3, which is 0.8967. These are illustrative design values, and their transparency is the point: a design whose arithmetic can be checked is a design that can be argued with. Formal statement: CF_combined = CF1 + CF2 - CF1 * CF2, for two positive certainty factors CF1 and CF2; mean combined = (sum of combined values) / n. The equation states the certainty-factor check the demo performs: it combines each pair of positive certainty factors by the MYCIN rule and averages the combined values.

Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of transparent uncertainty: a number for how sure the system is must be combined by a stated rule and reported with its provenance, or it is not a number but a gesture. The design follows the public evidence that certainty factors felt right to clinicians but were not probabilities, and it answers by treating the factors as scores with stated rules, never as probabilities. The design is a study, so its claims are design claims, and they are judged by whether the design is complete and coherent, not by whether a real system was measured. Scientific relation: A certainty factor states how strongly evidence supports a conclusion, from -1 for certain disbelief to 1 for certain belief; two pieces of supporting evidence combine to a stronger factor than either alone.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The engine is a design, not a system, so its factors have never been calibrated against real judgments, and its ceiling is a design choice rather than a measured threshold. The factors are scores, not probabilities, so they cannot be combined with statistical evidence. The design assumes the experts who assign the factors agree, which real experts often do not. The design is a study of how an engine should combine and report uncertainty, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such an engine exists or works.

Working demo program

import hashlib
cfs = [(0.7, 0.5), (0.6, 0.8), (0.8, 0.6)]
def compute():
    combined = [a + b - a * b for a, b in cfs]
    mean_combined = round(sum(combined) / len(combined), 4)
    n = len(cfs)
    out = {"mean_combined": mean_combined, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Signature Knowledge Elicitation Studio: A Design for Extracting Expertise JAH-ESYSS-000003 · 3

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Knowledge Elicitation Studio is a Signature-made design for the hardest part of building an expert system: getting the knowledge out of an expert's head and into a rule base. The design gives the knowledge engineer a studio, a set of tools for interviewing through cases, for testing rules against the expert's own judgments, and for tracking which rules are trusted and which are disputed. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of the knowledge-acquisition bottleneck, identified in the 1970s and never solved. The design takes the interview-test-refine cycle that knowledge engineers have always used, and gives it a structure: cases first, rules second, testing third, and a ledger of disagreements that drives the next interview. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Knowledge Elicitation Studio is a Signature-made design for the hardest part of building an expert system: getting the knowledge out of an expert's head and into a rule base. The design gives the knowledge engineer a studio, a set of tools for interviewing through cases, for testing rules against the expert's own judgments, and for tracking which rules are trusted and which are disputed. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature Knowledge Elicitation Studio, a Signature-made design-study for extracting expert knowledge, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: present the expert with cases, not abstract questions, because experts explain themselves best through cases. Step 2: write rules from the expert's answers, with the expert checking each one. Step 3: test the rules on fresh cases, and record every disagreement in the ledger. Step 4: return to the expert with the disagreements, refine the rules, and repeat. The demo runs a small worked example of the counting step with illustrative design values: it takes five elicitation rounds with rules written of 30, 45, 28, 52, and 36, and reports the total rules written, the mean per round, and the largest round. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"total_rules_design": 191, "mean_per_round_design": 38.2, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the studio contains a case library, a rule editor, a test harness, and a disagreement ledger, and the worked example shows the counting arithmetic. The five rounds write 30, 45, 28, 52, and 36 rules, for a total of 191, a mean of 38.2 per round, and a largest round of 52. The result that matters is the design judgment: a studio that tracks disagreements makes the bottleneck visible, and a bottleneck that is visible can be budgeted, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: largest: 52, mean: 38.2, n: 5, smallest: 28, total: 191.

When It Applies

The Signature Knowledge Elicitation Studio design applies as a study for anyone planning a rule-based system: it shows how to organize the elicitation, track the disagreements, and budget the rounds. It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply where no expert time is available, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Limits

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The studio is a design, not a system, so it has never been used with a real expert, and its rounds are illustrative. The design assumes the expert has time to give, which is the binding constraint in practice, and it does not solve the bottleneck, it only makes it visible. The design is a study of how elicitation should be organized, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such a studio exists or works.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of a ledger. The five rounds write 30, 45, 28, 52, and 36 rules, which sum to 191, and 191 divided by 5 is 38.2. The largest round writes 52 rules and the smallest writes 28. These are illustrative design values, and their transparency is the point: a design whose arithmetic can be checked is a design that can be argued with, and a design that cannot be checked is only a picture. Formal statement: total = sum of the counts; mean = total / n; smallest = min of the counts; largest = max of the counts. The equation states the ledger check the demo performs: it adds the named counts, divides by how many there are for the mean, and picks out the smallest and the largest.

Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of tacit knowledge: experts know more than they can say, and the studio's job is to make the unsaid visible through cases. The design follows the public evidence that interviewing through cases extracts more than interviewing through abstractions, and it adds the ledger, so that every disagreement is a task rather than a forgotten argument. The design is a study, so its claims are design claims, and they are judged by whether the design is complete and coherent, not by whether a real system was measured. import hashlib
ledger = [('round 1', 30), ('round 2', 45), ('round 3', 28), ('round 4', 52), ('round 5', 36)]
def compute():
    counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
    total = round(sum(counts), 4)
    mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
    smallest = min(counts)
    largest = max(counts)
    n = len(counts)
    out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Signature Diagnosis Shell: A Design for Narrow Medical Advice JAH-ESYSS-000004 · 4

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Diagnosis Shell is a Signature-made design for a narrow diagnostic adviser, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design covers one clinical area, with a bounded set of findings and a bounded set of diagnoses, and it advises rather than decides: it ranks the candidates, shows the findings behind each ranking, and leaves the decision to the clinician. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of medical expert systems from MYCIN in the 1970s through PUFF's routine use from 1979. The design takes the lesson of that history, that narrow systems reach the clinic and broad ones do not, and makes narrowness a requirement: the shell refuses to answer outside its stated scope, and it says so plainly when it does. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Working demo program

import hashlib
ledger = [('diagnosis 1', 0.85), ('diagnosis 2', 0.6), ('diagnosis 3', 0.78), ('diagnosis 4', 0.5), ('diagnosis 5', 0.7)]
def compute():
    counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
    total = round(sum(counts), 4)
    mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
    smallest = min(counts)
    largest = max(counts)
    n = len(counts)
    out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Signature Configuration Engine: A Design for Made-to-Order Products JAH-ESYSS-000005 · 5

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Configuration Engine is a Signature-made design for configuring made-to-order products from a closed catalogue, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design takes an order, checks it against compatibility rules, chooses the components and cabinets, and emits a parts list and assembly instructions, in the pattern XCON established at DEC. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of XCON, in service from 1978 and saving DEC about twenty-five million dollars a year by 1986. The design takes the configuration pattern XCON proved, rules for compatibility, search for a feasible configuration, and backtracking when a choice violates a constraint, and adds the maintenance ledger the original lacked: every rule carries its author, its date, and its revision count, so the rule base can be audited. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Configuration Engine is a Signature-made design for configuring made-to-order products from a closed catalogue, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design takes an order, checks it against compatibility rules, chooses the components and cabinets, and emits a parts list and assembly instructions, in the pattern XCON established at DEC. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature Configuration Engine, a Signature-made design-study for configuring products from a catalogue, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the order as a set of components the customer asked for. Step 2: match the configuration rules against the order, adding required components and choosing cabinets. Step 3: check every constraint, and backtrack when a choice violates one. Step 4: emit the configuration, the parts list, and the assembly instructions, with the rules that fired. The demo runs a small worked example of the checking step with illustrative design values: it takes five orders with component counts of 14, 9, 18, 11, and 16 against rule counts of 15, 10, 19, 12, and 17, and reports the mean absolute error and the root mean square error. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"mae_design": 1.0, "rmse_design": 1.0, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the engine configures, checks, backtracks, and audits, and the worked example shows the checking arithmetic. The gaps between the paired counts are 1, 1, 1, 1, and 1, giving a mean absolute error of 1.0 and a root mean square error of 1.0, with a largest gap of 1. The result that matters is the design judgment: a configuration engine that audits its rules can be maintained, and one that cannot will decay, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: mae: 1.0, max_err: 1, n: 5, rmse: 1.0.

It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply where the catalogue is open-ended, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of error measurement. The five gaps are 1, 1, 1, 1, and 1, which sum to 5, so the mean absolute error is 5 divided by 5, which is 1.0. The squared gaps are 1, 1, 1, 1, and 1, which sum to 5, so the mean squared error is 1.0 and the root mean square error is the square root of 1.0, which is 1.0. The largest single gap is 1. These are illustrative design values, and their transparency is the point: a design whose arithmetic can be checked is a design that can be argued with. Formal statement: MAE = (1/n) * sum |pred_i - actual_i|; RMSE = sqrt((1/n) * sum (pred_i - actual_i)^2); max error = max |pred_i - actual_i|. The equation states the forecast-error check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted and actual pairs, averages the absolute errors for the MAE, and takes the square root of the averaged squared errors for the RMSE.

The design follows the public evidence that XCON worked because the catalogue was closed and the rules were documented, and it adds the audit ledger because the public record also shows that the maintenance cost was the system's undoing. The design is a study, so its claims are design claims, and they are judged by whether the design is complete and coherent, not by whether a real system was measured. Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(14, 15), (9, 10), (18, 19), (11, 12), (16, 17)]
def compute():
    errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
    mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
    rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
    max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
    n = len(pairs)
    out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Working demo program

import hashlib
ledger = [('condition 1', 1), ('condition 2', 1), ('condition 3', 1), ('condition 4', 0), ('condition 5', 1)]
def compute():
    counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
    total = round(sum(counts), 4)
    mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
    smallest = min(counts)
    largest = max(counts)
    n = len(counts)
    out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class ProgJAHESYSS000006:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        ledger = [('condition 1', 1), ('condition 2', 1), ('condition 3', 1), ('condition 4', 0), ('condition 5', 1)]
        def compute():
            counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
            total = round(sum(counts), 4)
            mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
            smallest = min(counts)
            largest = max(counts)
            n = len(counts)
            out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(ProgJAHESYSS000006().run())

Signature Credit Rules Engine: A Design for Explainable Lending Policy JAH-ESYSS-000007 · 7

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Credit Rules Engine is a Signature-made design for applying a lending policy as rules, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design takes a written lending policy, encodes it as rules, and applies it consistently, reporting every rule that fired so the decision can be explained and appealed. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of financial expert systems from the 1980s and the FICO score from 1989. The design takes the lesson of that history, that a lending decision must be explainable, and makes explanation a requirement: every decision carries the rules that produced it, and every rule carries its source in the policy. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Credit Rules Engine is a Signature-made design for applying a lending policy as rules, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design takes a written lending policy, encodes it as rules, and applies it consistently, reporting every rule that fired so the decision can be explained and appealed. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature Credit Rules Engine, a Signature-made design-study for explainable lending policy, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the application as input, with its facts and amounts. Step 2: match the facts against the policy rules, checking each condition in turn. Step 3: decide from the rules that fired, and flag the application for human review when a rule requires it. Step 4: report the decision, the rules that fired, and the reasons. The demo runs a small worked example of the scoring step with illustrative design values: it takes five applications with policy scores of 0.78, 0.62, 0.85, 0.58, and 0.71 against reference scores of 0.8, 0.6, 0.85, 0.6, and 0.7, and reports the mean absolute error and the root mean square error. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"mae_design": 0.014, "rmse_design": 0.0161, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the engine applies the policy, explains the decision, and flags the cases for review, and the worked example shows the scoring arithmetic. The gaps are 0.02, 0.02, 0.0, 0.02, and 0.01, giving a mean absolute error of 0.014 and a root mean square error of 0.0161, with a largest gap of 0.02. The result that matters is the design judgment: a lending engine that explains itself can be audited, and one that cannot will reproduce its policy's biases unseen, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: mae: 0.014, max_err: 0.02, n: 5, rmse: 0.0161.

When It Applies

The Signature Credit Rules Engine design applies as a study for anyone building a lending system: it shows how to encode, explain, and audit a policy, and gives the arithmetic for checking the work. It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply where the policy is unwritten or unaudited, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Limits

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The engine is a design, not a system, so it has never decided a real application, and its scores are illustrative. The design assumes the policy is written down and fair, which real policies often are not, and it cannot detect a pattern nobody described. The design is a study of how a lending engine should be built and audited, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such an engine exists or works.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of error measurement. The five gaps are 0.02, 0.02, 0.0, 0.02, and 0.01, which sum to 0.07, so the mean absolute error is 0.07 divided by 5, which is 0.014. The squared gaps are 0.0004, 0.0004, 0.0, 0.0004, and 0.0001, which sum to 0.0013, so the mean squared error is 0.00026 and the root mean square error is the square root of 0.00026, which is 0.0161. The largest single gap is 0.02. These are illustrative design values, and their transparency is the point: a design whose arithmetic can be checked is a design that can be argued with. Formal statement: MAE = (1/n) * sum |pred_i - actual_i|; RMSE = sqrt((1/n) * sum (pred_i - actual_i)^2); max error = max |pred_i - actual_i|. The equation states the forecast-error check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted and actual pairs, averages the absolute errors for the MAE, and takes the square root of the averaged squared errors for the RMSE.

Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of policy as code: a lending policy is a document, and a rule base is a document made executable, so the decision is the policy applied consistently. The design follows the public evidence that financial rules survive because they are explainable, and it adds the audit requirement, that the engine's decisions be checked for bias as carefully as for accuracy. The design is a study, so its claims are design claims, and they are judged by whether the design is complete and coherent, not by whether a real system was measured. Scientific relation: A forecast is judged by the gap between what was predicted and what happened; the MAE averages that gap and the RMSE punishes large gaps more heavily.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(0.78, 0.8), (0.62, 0.6), (0.85, 0.85), (0.58, 0.6), (0.71, 0.7)]
def compute():
    errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
    mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
    rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
    max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
    n = len(pairs)
    out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Signature Fault Diagnosis Shell: A Design for Industrial Troubleshooting JAH-ESYSS-000008 · 8

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Fault Diagnosis Shell is a Signature-made design for diagnosing faults in industrial equipment, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design takes sensor readings and operator reports, matches them against fault rules written from the maintenance manual, and ranks the candidate faults with the evidence for each. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of industrial expert systems from XCON in 1978 through the fault-diagnosis systems of the 1980s. The design takes the lesson of that history, that industrial knowledge is documented in manuals, and makes the manual the source: every rule carries its page in the manual, so the diagnosis can be checked against the book. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Fault Diagnosis Shell is a Signature-made design for diagnosing faults in industrial equipment, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design takes sensor readings and operator reports, matches them against fault rules written from the maintenance manual, and ranks the candidate faults with the evidence for each. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature Fault Diagnosis Shell, a Signature-made design-study for industrial troubleshooting, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the sensor readings and operator reports as input. Step 2: match the readings against the fault rules, checking each symptom pattern in turn. Step 3: rank the candidate faults by how many symptoms match and how strongly. Step 4: report the leading fault, the evidence for it, and the recommended action, with the manual pages cited. The demo runs a small worked example of the ranking step with illustrative design values: it takes five candidate faults with match counts of 8, 13, 6, 11, and 9, and reports the total, the mean, the smallest, and the largest. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"total_matches_design": 47, "mean_matches_design": 9.4, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the shell matches, ranks, and cites its manual pages, and the worked example shows the ranking arithmetic. The five match counts 8, 13, 6, 11, and 9 total 47, with a mean of 9.4, a smallest of 6, and a largest of 13. The result that matters is the design judgment: a diagnosis that cites its manual can be checked by the operator, and one that does not cannot, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: largest: 13, mean: 9.4, n: 5, smallest: 6, total: 47.

When It Applies

The Signature Fault Diagnosis Shell design applies as a study for anyone building an industrial diagnostic: it shows how to match, rank, and cite, and gives the arithmetic for checking the work. It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply where the manual is incomplete, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Limits

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The shell is a design, not a system, so it has never diagnosed a real fault, and its counts are illustrative. The design assumes the manual is complete, which real manuals are not, and it cannot diagnose a fault the manual does not describe. A sensor fault can also mislead it, because a rule that trusts a broken sensor will reach a wrong conclusion confidently. Working demo program

import hashlib
ledger = [('fault 1', 8), ('fault 2', 13), ('fault 3', 6), ('fault 4', 11), ('fault 5', 9)]
def compute():
    counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
    total = round(sum(counts), 4)
    mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
    smallest = min(counts)
    largest = max(counts)
    n = len(counts)
    out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class ProgJAHESYSS000008:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        ledger = [('fault 1', 8), ('fault 2', 13), ('fault 3', 6), ('fault 4', 11), ('fault 5', 9)]
        def compute():
            counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
            total = round(sum(counts), 4)
            mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
            smallest = min(counts)
            largest = max(counts)
            n = len(counts)
            out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(ProgJAHESYSS000008().run())

Signature Explanation Facility: A Design for Showing the Reasoning JAH-ESYSS-000009 · 9

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Explanation Facility is a Signature-made design for showing the reasoning behind an expert system's conclusion, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design answers the two questions MYCIN's facility answered, how a conclusion was reached and why a question was asked, and it adds a limit: no explanation may show more than twelve rules, so that the chain stays readable. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of explanation facilities from MYCIN in the 1970s. The design takes the how-trace and the why-trace the public record describes, and adds the readability limit, because a chain of seventy rules is no more understandable than no chain at all. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Explanation Facility is a Signature-made design for showing the reasoning behind an expert system's conclusion, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design answers the two questions MYCIN's facility answered, how a conclusion was reached and why a question was asked, and it adds a limit: no explanation may show more than twelve rules, so that the chain stays readable. The Problem

What is the Signature Explanation Facility, a Signature-made design-study for showing how a conclusion was reached, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: record every rule that fires, with the facts that satisfied it and the conclusion it produced. Step 2: when asked how, walk the recorded chain backward from the conclusion to the facts. Step 3: when asked why, show the rule that needs the fact being requested, and the goal that rule serves. Step 4: present the chain in plain language, and refuse to show a chain longer than twelve rules, summarizing it instead. The demo runs a small worked example of the tracing step with illustrative design values: it takes five explanation chains with rules shown of 5, 9, 4, 8, and 6, and reports the total, the mean, the smallest, and the largest. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the facility traces how and why, and caps the chain at twelve rules, and the worked example shows the tracing arithmetic. The five chains show 5, 9, 4, 8, and 6 rules, for a total of 32, a mean of 6.4, a smallest of 4, and a largest of 9. The result that matters is the design judgment: an explanation of six rules can be read and checked, and the twelve-rule cap is what keeps it readable, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: largest: 9, mean: 6.4, n: 5, smallest: 4, total: 32.

It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply as a guarantee of correctness, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Signature RETE Engine: A Design for Fast Pattern Matching JAH-ESYSS-000010 · 10

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature RETE Engine is a Signature-made design for fast pattern matching in a rule-based system, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design takes the RETE algorithm of 1979, which remembers partial matches in a network and updates them only when the facts change, and gives it a modern form: the network is built from the rules, the facts flow through it, and the engine reports how many nodes each cycle visited, so the cost is visible. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of the RETE algorithm, published by Charles Forgy in 1979 and used in OPS5, CLIPS, and Drools. The design takes the algorithm's core idea, that most facts do not change on most cycles, and makes the accounting explicit: every cycle reports the nodes visited, so the efficiency can be checked rather than assumed. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature RETE Engine is a Signature-made design for fast pattern matching in a rule-based system, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design takes the RETE algorithm of 1979, which remembers partial matches in a network and updates them only when the facts change, and gives it a modern form: the network is built from the rules, the facts flow through it, and the engine reports how many nodes each cycle visited, so the cost is visible. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature RETE Engine, a Signature-made design-study for fast rule matching, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: compile the rules into a RETE network, with nodes that test single patterns and nodes that join patterns together. Step 2: feed the facts into the network, letting each fact flow through the nodes that test it. Step 3: remember the partial matches at each node, so a new fact only re-tests the paths it touches. Step 4: fire the rules whose patterns are fully matched, and report the nodes visited. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of incremental computation: most facts do not change on most cycles, so recomputing everything is waste, and the algorithm trades memory for time by remembering partial matches. History

The design takes the algorithm's core idea, that most facts do not change on most cycles, and makes the accounting explicit: every cycle reports the nodes visited, so the efficiency can be checked rather than assumed. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

The demo runs a small worked example of the matching step with illustrative design values: it takes five cycles with nodes visited of 95, 70, 110, 80, and 90, and reports the total, the mean, the smallest, and the largest. Working demo program

import hashlib
ledger = [('cycle 1', 95), ('cycle 2', 70), ('cycle 3', 110), ('cycle 4', 80), ('cycle 5', 90)]
def compute():
    counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
    total = round(sum(counts), 4)
    mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
    smallest = min(counts)
    largest = max(counts)
    n = len(counts)
    out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class ProgJAHESYSS000010:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        ledger = [('cycle 1', 95), ('cycle 2', 70), ('cycle 3', 110), ('cycle 4', 80), ('cycle 5', 90)]
        def compute():
            counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
            total = round(sum(counts), 4)
            mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
            smallest = min(counts)
            largest = max(counts)
            n = len(counts)
            out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(ProgJAHESYSS000010().run())

Signature Frame Knowledge Base: A Design for Objects and Defaults JAH-ESYSS-000011 · 11

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Frame Knowledge Base is a Signature-made design for representing objects with frames, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design takes Minsky's 1974 frame idea, an object packaged with its slots and default values, and gives it a modern form: every frame states its slots, its defaults, and the conditions under which a default may be overridden, so that the knowledge is explicit and checkable. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of frames from Minsky 1974 and semantic networks from 1968. The design takes the frame's strength, that it packages what is typical about an object, and addresses its weakness, that defaults can be wrong, by requiring every default to carry its source and its override condition. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Frame Knowledge Base is a Signature-made design for representing objects with frames, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design takes Minsky's 1974 frame idea, an object packaged with its slots and default values, and gives it a modern form: every frame states its slots, its defaults, and the conditions under which a default may be overridden, so that the knowledge is explicit and checkable. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature Frame Knowledge Base, a Signature-made design-study for frames and defaults, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the domain's objects and name them, with definitions a human can check. Step 2: write each object as a frame, with slots for its properties and defaults for the typical values. Step 3: state the override condition for every default, so the system knows when the typical value does not apply. Step 4: test the frames by asking the questions the system must answer, and revise where they cannot answer. The demo runs a small worked example of the counting step with illustrative design values: it takes five frame groups with frames defined of 45, 60, 38, 72, and 51, and reports the total, the mean, the smallest, and the largest. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The result that matters is the design judgment: a frame base that states its defaults and overrides can be checked, and one that hides them cannot, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. import hashlib
ledger = [('group 1', 45), ('group 2', 60), ('group 3', 38), ('group 4', 72), ('group 5', 51)]
def compute():
    counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
    total = round(sum(counts), 4)
    mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
    smallest = min(counts)
    largest = max(counts)
    n = len(counts)
    out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()

h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Signature Backward Chaining Engine: A Design for Goal-Driven Reasoning JAH-ESYSS-000012 · 12

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Backward Chaining Engine is a Signature-made design for goal-driven inference, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design takes the MYCIN pattern, start with the goal and work back to the evidence, and gives it a modern form: the search is depth-first with loop detection, every subgoal is cached so it is never recomputed, and the chain is reported as the explanation. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of backward chaining from MYCIN in the 1970s and Prolog in 1972. The design takes the strategy's strength, its focus on the goal, and addresses its known weaknesses, looping on circular rules and recomputing the same subgoal, by adding loop detection and caching to the design. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Backward Chaining Engine is a Signature-made design for goal-driven inference, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design takes the MYCIN pattern, start with the goal and work back to the evidence, and gives it a modern form: the search is depth-first with loop detection, every subgoal is cached so it is never recomputed, and the chain is reported as the explanation. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature Backward Chaining Engine, a Signature-made design-study for goal-driven inference, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: state the goal to be proved. Step 2: find the rules whose conclusions match the goal. Step 3: try to prove each rule's premises, recursing into subgoals, asking the user where a premise is a fact, and checking the cache before recomputing. Step 4: succeed when a chain reaches known facts, fail when every chain fails, and report the chain as the explanation. The demo runs a small worked example of the search with illustrative design values: it takes five goal attempts with subgoals explored of 7, 12, 5, 10, and 8, and reports the total subgoals explored, the mean per attempt, and the largest attempt. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"total_subgoals_design": 42, "mean_per_attempt_design": 8.4, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the engine detects loops, caches subgoals, and reports the chain, and the worked example shows the search arithmetic. The five attempts explore 7, 12, 5, 10, and 8 subgoals, for a total of 42, a mean of 8.4 per attempt, and a largest attempt of 12. The result that matters is the design judgment: a goal-driven engine that caches and detects loops is cheaper than one that does neither, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: largest: 12, mean: 8.4, n: 5, smallest: 5, total: 42.

When It Applies

The Signature Backward Chaining Engine design applies as a study for anyone building a goal-driven system: it shows how to search, cache, and detect loops, and gives the arithmetic for checking the work. It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply where the goal is broad, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Limits

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The engine is a design, not a system, so it has never proved a real goal, and its subgoal counts are illustrative. The design assumes the goal is narrow and the rules are acyclic enough for loop detection to help, which is not always true. The design is a study of how a goal-driven engine should be built, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such an engine exists or works.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of a search tree. The five attempts explore 7, 12, 5, 10, and 8 subgoals, which sum to 42, and 42 divided by 5 is 8.4. The largest attempt explores 12 subgoals and the smallest explores 5. These are illustrative design values, and their transparency is the point: a design whose arithmetic can be checked is a design that can be argued with, and a design that cannot be checked is only a picture. Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of goal-directed search: a question defines a tree of subquestions, and the strategy explores that tree, asking for facts only when it needs them. The design follows the public evidence that backward chaining is cheap when the goal is narrow and expensive when it is broad, and it adds the caching and loop detection that the public record shows the strategy needs. The design is a study, so its claims are design claims, and they are judged by whether the design is complete and coherent, not by whether a real system was measured. Working demo program

import hashlib
ledger = [('attempt 1', 7), ('attempt 2', 12), ('attempt 3', 5), ('attempt 4', 10), ('attempt 5', 8)]
def compute():
    counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
    total = round(sum(counts), 4)
    mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
    smallest = min(counts)
    largest = max(counts)
    n = len(counts)
    out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class ProgJAHESYSS000012:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        ledger = [('attempt 1', 7), ('attempt 2', 12), ('attempt 3', 5), ('attempt 4', 10), ('attempt 5', 8)]
        def compute():
            counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
            total = round(sum(counts), 4)
            mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
            smallest = min(counts)
            largest = max(counts)
            n = len(counts)
            out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(ProgJAHESYSS000012().run())

Signature Forward Chaining Engine: A Design for Data-Driven Reasoning JAH-ESYSS-000013 · 13

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Forward Chaining Engine is a Signature-made design for data-driven inference, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design takes the CLIPS pattern, start with the facts and derive everything that follows, and gives it a modern form: the engine derives the closure, ranks the conclusions by relevance to the task, and stops when the new facts fall below a stated threshold, so the user is not buried in noise. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of forward chaining from the production systems of the 1970s through CLIPS in 1985. The design takes the strategy's strength, its completeness within its rules, and addresses its known weakness, that it derives conclusions nobody asked for, by adding relevance ranking and a stopping threshold to the design. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

import hashlib
ledger = [('cycle 1', 11), ('cycle 2', 7), ('cycle 3', 16), ('cycle 4', 5), ('cycle 5', 9)]
def compute():
    counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
    total = round(sum(counts), 4)
    mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
    smallest = min(counts)
    largest = max(counts)
    n = len(counts)
    out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class ProgJAHESYSS000013:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        ledger = [('cycle 1', 11), ('cycle 2', 7), ('cycle 3', 16), ('cycle 4', 5), ('cycle 5', 9)]
        def compute():
            counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
            total = round(sum(counts), 4)
            mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
            smallest = min(counts)
            largest = max(counts)
            n = len(counts)
            out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
            return out

        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(ProgJAHESYSS000013().run())
