Signature Hybrid Rule-Learner: A Design for Rules That Learn JAH-ESYSS-000014 · 14

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Hybrid Rule-Learner is a Signature-made design for combining rules with learning, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design keeps a rule base for the policy that must be explained, and a learned model for the patterns that must be discovered, and it gives the two a protocol: the model proposes, the rules constrain, and the human decides. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of the comparison between rule-based systems and machine learning, from the 1980s through the learning revival of the 2010s. The design takes the hybrid position the public record supports, rules for the explainable policy and learning for the discoverable pattern, and makes it an architecture with a stated protocol. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Hybrid Rule-Learner is a Signature-made design for combining rules with learning, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design keeps a rule base for the policy that must be explained, and a learned model for the patterns that must be discovered, and it gives the two a protocol: the model proposes, the rules constrain, and the human decides. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature Hybrid Rule-Learner, a Signature-made design-study for combining rules with learning, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: write the policy as rules, and train the model on the data. Step 2: let the model propose its answer, with its confidence. Step 3: let the rules check the proposal, rejecting it when it violates the policy and passing it when it does not. Step 4: report the proposal, the rule check, and the decision, with the human in the loop. The demo runs a small worked example of the comparison step with illustrative design values: it takes five tasks with rule-checked accuracy of 0.86, 0.74, 0.9, 0.68, and 0.8 against model-only accuracy of 0.88, 0.72, 0.92, 0.7, and 0.82, and reports the mean absolute gap and the root mean square gap. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"mae_gap_design": 0.02, "rmse_gap_design": 0.02, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the hybrid proposes, constrains, and decides, and the worked example shows the comparison arithmetic. The gaps are 0.02, 0.02, 0.02, 0.02, and 0.02, giving a mean absolute gap of 0.02 and a root mean square gap of 0.02, with a largest gap of 0.02. The result that matters is the design judgment: a hybrid that constrains the model with rules is auditable, and a model alone is not, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: mae: 0.02, max_err: 0.02, n: 5, rmse: 0.02.

When It Applies

The Signature Hybrid Rule-Learner design applies as a study for anyone combining rules with learning: it shows how to propose, constrain, and decide, and gives the arithmetic for checking the work. It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply where the policy cannot be written, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Limits

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The hybrid is a design, not a system, so it has never decided a real task, and its accuracies are illustrative. The design assumes the policy can be written as rules and the model can be trained, which is not always true, and the protocol assumes the human will check the decision, which is not always true either. The design is a study of how a hybrid should be built, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such a hybrid exists or works.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of error measurement. The five gaps are 0.02, 0.02, 0.02, 0.02, and 0.02, which sum to 0.1, so the mean absolute gap is 0.1 divided by 5, which is 0.02. The squared gaps are 0.0004, 0.0004, 0.0004, 0.0004, and 0.0004, which sum to 0.002, so the mean squared gap is 0.0004 and the root mean square gap is the square root of 0.0004, which is 0.02. The largest single gap is 0.02. These are illustrative design values, and their transparency is the point: a design whose arithmetic can be checked is a design that can be argued with. Formal statement: MAE = (1/n) * sum |pred_i - actual_i|; RMSE = sqrt((1/n) * sum (pred_i - actual_i)^2); max error = max |pred_i - actual_i|. The equation states the forecast-error check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted and actual pairs, averages the absolute errors for the MAE, and takes the square root of the averaged squared errors for the RMSE.

Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of combining explicit and implicit knowledge: rules represent knowledge a human can read, and learned models represent knowledge no human can read, and the hybrid uses each where it is strong. The design follows the public evidence that the hybrid position is the standard in industry, and it makes the protocol explicit, so that the division of labour can be checked. The design is a study, so its claims are design claims, and they are judged by whether the design is complete and coherent, not by whether a real system was measured. Scientific relation: A forecast is judged by the gap between what was predicted and what happened; the MAE averages that gap and the RMSE punishes large gaps more heavily.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(0.86, 0.88), (0.74, 0.72), (0.9, 0.92), (0.68, 0.7), (0.8, 0.82)]
def compute():
    errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
    mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
    rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
    max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
    n = len(pairs)
    out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class ProgJAHESYSS000014:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        pairs = [(0.86, 0.88), (0.74, 0.72), (0.9, 0.92), (0.68, 0.7), (0.8, 0.82)]
        def compute():
            errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
            mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
            rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
            max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
            n = len(pairs)
            out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(ProgJAHESYSS000014().run())

Signature Validation Harness: A Design for Testing Rule Bases JAH-ESYSS-000015 · 15

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Validation Harness is a Signature-made design for testing a rule base, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design gives the tester three tools: a blinded comparison against experts on fresh cases, a consistency check that finds rules that never fire and rules that contradict each other, and a coverage check that finds the cases no rule covers. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of validation from MYCIN's 1979 blinded evaluation through the consistency checks developed for XCON. The design takes the two questions the public record separates, whether the system is right and whether it is built correctly, and gives each a tool, so that validation and verification are both covered. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Validation Harness is a Signature-made design for testing a rule base, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design gives the tester three tools: a blinded comparison against experts on fresh cases, a consistency check that finds rules that never fire and rules that contradict each other, and a coverage check that finds the cases no rule covers. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature Validation Harness, a Signature-made design-study for testing rule bases, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: assemble test cases from real practice, with the expert's conclusions recorded before the system runs. Step 2: run the system blinded, and compare its conclusions with the expert's. Step 3: check the rule base for consistency, finding the rules that never fire, the rules that contradict each other, and the gaps where no rule covers a case. Step 4: report the agreement, the contradictions, and the gaps, and return the disagreements to the expert. The demo runs a small worked example of the comparison step with illustrative design values: it takes five test batches with agreement rates of 0.88, 0.82, 0.9, 0.78, and 0.85 against reference rates of 0.88, 0.84, 0.88, 0.8, and 0.85, and reports the mean absolute error and the root mean square error. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"mae_design": 0.012, "rmse_design": 0.0155, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the harness compares, checks consistency, and checks coverage, and the worked example shows the comparison arithmetic. The gaps are 0.0, 0.02, 0.02, 0.02, and 0.0, giving a mean absolute error of 0.012 and a root mean square error of 0.0155, with a largest gap of 0.02. The result that matters is the design judgment: a harness that checks all three, agreement, consistency, and coverage, is a complete test, and one that checks only agreement is not, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: mae: 0.012, max_err: 0.02, n: 5, rmse: 0.0155.

When It Applies

The Signature Validation Harness design applies as a study for anyone testing a rule-based system: it shows how to compare, check consistency, and check coverage. It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply where no fresh cases exist, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Limits

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The harness is a design, not a system, so it has never tested a real rule base, and its rates are illustrative. The design assumes fresh cases and independent judges are available, which they often are not, and it cannot find a rule that is wrong in a way that is consistent with the rest of the base. The design is a study of how a rule base should be tested, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such a harness exists or works.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of error measurement. The five gaps are 0.0, 0.02, 0.02, 0.02, and 0.0, which sum to 0.06, so the mean absolute error is 0.06 divided by 5, which is 0.012. The largest single gap is 0.02. These are illustrative design values, and their transparency is the point: a design whose arithmetic can be checked is a design that can be argued with. The equation states the forecast-error check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted and actual pairs, averages the absolute errors for the MAE, and takes the square root of the averaged squared errors for the RMSE.

Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of honest evaluation: a system tested on the cases it was built from will pass, and a system tested by its own author will pass, so the test must use fresh cases and independent judges. The design follows the public evidence that MYCIN's blinded evaluation is the model, and it adds the consistency and coverage checks, because agreement alone cannot find a rule base that is wrong in a consistent way. The design is a study, so its claims are design claims, and they are judged by whether the design is complete and coherent, not by whether a real system was measured. Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(0.88, 0.88), (0.82, 0.84), (0.9, 0.88), (0.78, 0.8), (0.85, 0.85)]
def compute():
    errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
    mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
    rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
    max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
    n = len(pairs)
    out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

class ProgJAHESYSS000015:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        pairs = [(0.88, 0.88), (0.82, 0.84), (0.9, 0.88), (0.78, 0.8), (0.85, 0.85)]
        def compute():

            mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
            rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)

            n = len(pairs)
            out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()

        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")

Signature Ontology Builder: A Design for Shared Vocabularies JAH-ESYSS-000016 · 16

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Ontology Builder is a Signature-made design for building a shared vocabulary for a domain, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design gives the builder a method: name the concepts, define them in plain language, link them by relationships, and test the result by asking the questions the vocabulary must answer. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of ontology engineering from the 1990s and the OWL standard of 2004. The design takes the discipline the public record shows, that a vocabulary must be defined, linked, and tested, and makes it a workflow with a count at each step, so the builder can see the size of what is being built. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Ontology Builder is a Signature-made design for building a shared vocabulary for a domain, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design gives the builder a method: name the concepts, define them in plain language, link them by relationships, and test the result by asking the questions the vocabulary must answer. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature Ontology Builder, a Signature-made design-study for building shared vocabularies, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the domain's concepts and name them, with definitions a human can check. Step 2: link the concepts by relationships, stating what each relationship means. Step 3: test the vocabulary by asking the questions it must answer, and revise where it cannot answer. Step 4: publish the vocabulary with its version, its definitions, and its test questions. The demo runs a small worked example of the counting step with illustrative design values: it takes five vocabulary layers with concepts defined of 210, 340, 180, 420, and 290, and reports the total, the mean, the smallest, and the largest. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"total_concepts_design": 1440, "mean_concepts_design": 288.0, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the builder names, defines, links, and tests, and the worked example shows the counting arithmetic. The five layers define 210, 340, 180, 420, and 290 concepts, for a total of 1440, a mean of 288.0, a smallest of 180, and a largest of 420. The result that matters is the design judgment: a vocabulary that can be counted can be managed, and one that cannot be counted cannot, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: largest: 420, mean: 288.0, n: 5, smallest: 180, total: 1440.

When It Applies

The Signature Ontology Builder design applies as a study for anyone building a shared vocabulary: it shows how to name, define, link, and test, and gives the arithmetic for checking the work. It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply where the concepts cannot be defined, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Limits

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The builder is a design, not a system, so its vocabularies have never been used by real systems, and its counts are illustrative. The design assumes the concepts are stable enough to define, which is not always true, and it cannot handle a domain where the concepts change faster than they can be defined. The design is a study of how a vocabulary should be built, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such a builder exists or works.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of a ledger. The five layers define 210, 340, 180, 420, and 290 concepts, which sum to 1440, and 1440 divided by 5 is 288.0. The smallest layer defines 180 concepts and the largest defines 420. These are illustrative design values, and their transparency is the point: a design whose arithmetic can be checked is a design that can be argued with, and a design that cannot be checked is only a picture. Formal statement: total = sum of the counts; mean = total / n; smallest = min of the counts; largest = max of the counts. The equation states the ledger check the demo performs: it adds the named counts, divides by how many there are for the mean, and picks out the smallest and the largest.

Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of shared meaning: different systems can only agree when they share a vocabulary, and a vocabulary is only shared when its concepts are defined, linked, and tested. The design follows the public evidence that the ontologies that survived were the ones whose concepts were defined and tested, and it makes the testing part of the workflow rather than an afterthought. The design is a study, so its claims are design claims, and they are judged by whether the design is complete and coherent, not by whether a real system was measured. import hashlib
ledger = [('layer 1', 210), ('layer 2', 340), ('layer 3', 180), ('layer 4', 420), ('layer 5', 290)]
def compute():
    counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
    total = round(sum(counts), 4)
    mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
    smallest = min(counts)
    largest = max(counts)
    n = len(counts)
    out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Signature Decision Table Studio: A Design for Decisions as Tables JAH-ESYSS-000017 · 17

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Decision Table Studio is a Signature-made design for writing decisions as tables, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design takes the decision-table idea, that a decision with several conditions is clearest as a table of combinations and outcomes, and gives it a studio: the analyst writes the conditions, the studio checks that every combination is covered and no two rows contradict each other, and the table is published as the decision. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of decision tables and the Decision Model and Notation standard of 2015. The design takes the table's strength, that it shows every combination, and addresses its weakness, that tables grow large, by requiring the studio to check coverage and contradiction automatically. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Decision Table Studio is a Signature-made design for writing decisions as tables, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design takes the decision-table idea, that a decision with several conditions is clearest as a table of combinations and outcomes, and gives it a studio: the analyst writes the conditions, the studio checks that every combination is covered and no two rows contradict each other, and the table is published as the decision. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature Decision Table Studio, a Signature-made design-study for writing decisions as tables, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: state the conditions and the outcomes of the decision. Step 2: write the table, with one row per combination of conditions and the outcome for each. Step 3: let the studio check the table for coverage, that every combination has a row, and for contradiction, that no two rows with the same conditions give different outcomes. Step 4: publish the table with its checks, so the decision can be audited. The demo runs a small worked example of the counting step with illustrative design values: it takes five decision tables with rows of 16, 24, 12, 32, and 20, and reports the total, the mean, the smallest, and the largest. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The five tables have 16, 24, 12, 32, and 20 rows, for a total of 104, a mean of 20.8, a smallest of 12, and a largest of 32. The result that matters is the design judgment: a table that is checked for coverage and contradiction can be trusted, and one that is not checked cannot, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: largest: 32, mean: 20.8, n: 5, smallest: 12, total: 104.

It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply where the conditions cannot be enumerated, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The studio is a design, not a system, so its tables have never decided a real case, and its row counts are illustrative. The design assumes the conditions can be enumerated, which is not always true, and a table with many conditions grows too large to read. The design is a study of how decisions should be written as tables, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such a studio exists or works.

Working demo program

import hashlib
ledger = [('table 1', 16), ('table 2', 24), ('table 3', 12), ('table 4', 32), ('table 5', 20)]
def compute():
    counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
    total = round(sum(counts), 4)
    mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
    smallest = min(counts)
    largest = max(counts)
    n = len(counts)
    out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class ProgJAHESYSS000017:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        ledger = [('table 1', 16), ('table 2', 24), ('table 3', 12), ('table 4', 32), ('table 5', 20)]
        def compute():
            counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
            total = round(sum(counts), 4)
            mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
            smallest = min(counts)
            largest = max(counts)
            n = len(counts)
            out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(ProgJAHESYSS000017().run())

Signature Fuzzy Rule Shell: A Design for Vague Boundaries JAH-ESYSS-000018 · 18

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Fuzzy Rule Shell is a Signature-made design for rules whose boundaries are vague, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design takes the fuzzy idea, that a temperature can be warm to a degree rather than warm or not, and gives it a shell: the membership functions are stated, the rules fire to a degree, and the conclusion is defuzzified into a crisp action, with every step shown. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of fuzzy logic, from Lotfi Zadeh's 1965 paper through the fuzzy control systems of the 1980s. The design takes the fuzzy strength, that it handles boundaries that are genuinely vague, and addresses its weakness, that the membership functions are judgments, by requiring every function to be stated with its source. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Fuzzy Rule Shell is a Signature-made design for rules whose boundaries are vague, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design takes the fuzzy idea, that a temperature can be warm to a degree rather than warm or not, and gives it a shell: the membership functions are stated, the rules fire to a degree, and the conclusion is defuzzified into a crisp action, with every step shown. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature Fuzzy Rule Shell, a Signature-made design-study for rules with vague boundaries, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: state the membership functions, the curves that say how much a value belongs to each term. Step 2: write the rules in terms of those memberships, so a rule can fire to a degree. Step 3: combine the fired rules, weighting each by its degree. Step 4: defuzzify the combination into a crisp action, and report the memberships, the rules, and the result. The demo runs a small worked example of the scoring step with illustrative design values: it takes five fuzzy outputs with membership degrees of 0.9, 0.7, 0.85, 0.6, and 0.75 against reference degrees of 0.88, 0.72, 0.83, 0.62, and 0.74, and reports the mean absolute error and the root mean square error. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"mae_design": 0.018, "rmse_design": 0.0184, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the shell states memberships, fires to a degree, and defuzzifies, and the worked example shows the scoring arithmetic. The gaps are 0.02, 0.02, 0.02, 0.02, and 0.01, giving a mean absolute error of 0.018 and a root mean square error of 0.0184, with a largest gap of 0.02. The result that matters is the design judgment: a fuzzy shell that states its membership functions can be argued with, and one that hides them cannot, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: mae: 0.018, max_err: 0.02, n: 5, rmse: 0.0184.

When It Applies

The Signature Fuzzy Rule Shell design applies as a study for anyone building a system with vague boundaries: it shows how to state memberships, fire to a degree, and defuzzify. It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply where the boundaries are crisp, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Limits

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The shell is a design, not a system, so it has never controlled a real process, and its degrees are illustrative. The design assumes the membership functions can be stated, which is a judgment, and it cannot handle a problem where the vagueness is in the rules rather than in the terms. The design is a study of how a fuzzy shell should be built, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such a shell exists or works.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of error measurement. The five gaps are 0.02, 0.02, 0.02, 0.02, and 0.01, which sum to 0.09, so the mean absolute error is 0.09 divided by 5, which is 0.018. The squared gaps are 0.0004, 0.0004, 0.0004, 0.0004, and 0.0001, which sum to 0.0017, so the mean squared error is 0.00034 and the root mean square error is the square root of 0.00034, which is 0.0184. The largest single gap is 0.02. These are illustrative design values, and their transparency is the point: a design whose arithmetic can be checked is a design that can be argued with. Formal statement: MAE = (1/n) * sum |pred_i - actual_i|; RMSE = sqrt((1/n) * sum (pred_i - actual_i)^2); max error = max |pred_i - actual_i|. The equation states the forecast-error check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted and actual pairs, averages the absolute errors for the MAE, and takes the square root of the averaged squared errors for the RMSE.

Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of vagueness: some boundaries are genuinely vague, and a system that forces them to be crisp will be wrong at the boundary. The design follows the public evidence that fuzzy control worked where the boundaries were vague and the rules were few, and it adds the requirement that the membership functions be stated with their source. The design is a study, so its claims are design claims, and they are judged by whether the design is complete and coherent, not by whether a real system was measured. Scientific relation: A forecast is judged by the gap between what was predicted and what happened; the MAE averages that gap and the RMSE punishes large gaps more heavily.. Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(0.9, 0.88), (0.7, 0.72), (0.85, 0.83), (0.6, 0.62), (0.75, 0.74)]
def compute():
    errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
    mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
    rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
    max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
    n = len(pairs)
    out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Working demo program

import hashlib
ledger = [('window 1', 14), ('window 2', 20), ('window 3', 11), ('window 4', 17), ('window 5', 13)]
def compute():
    counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
    total = round(sum(counts), 4)
    mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
    smallest = min(counts)
    largest = max(counts)
    n = len(counts)
    out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class ProgJAHESYSS000019:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        ledger = [('window 1', 14), ('window 2', 20), ('window 3', 11), ('window 4', 17), ('window 5', 13)]
        def compute():
            counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
            total = round(sum(counts), 4)
            mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
            smallest = min(counts)
            largest = max(counts)
            n = len(counts)
            out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(ProgJAHESYSS000019().run())

Signature Multi-Expert Merger: A Design for Combining Experts JAH-ESYSS-000020 · 20

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Multi-Expert Merger is a Signature-made design for combining the rules of several experts into one rule base, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design takes the problem the public record shows, that experts disagree, and gives it a method: each expert's rules are kept in a separate module, the disagreements are listed, and the merger resolves them by the stated rule, seniority, evidence, or a vote, with every resolution recorded. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of knowledge acquisition, where the bottleneck was always the expert's time and the disagreements between experts. The design takes the modular idea, that each expert's knowledge should be kept separate until it is merged, and makes the merger explicit, so that no disagreement is resolved silently. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Multi-Expert Merger is a Signature-made design for combining the rules of several experts into one rule base, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design takes the problem the public record shows, that experts disagree, and gives it a method: each expert's rules are kept in a separate module, the disagreements are listed, and the merger resolves them by the stated rule, seniority, evidence, or a vote, with every resolution recorded. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature Multi-Expert Merger, a Signature-made design-study for combining several experts' rules, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: keep each expert's rules in a separate module, with the expert's name on every rule. Step 2: list the disagreements, the cases where the modules give different answers. Step 3: resolve each disagreement by the stated rule, and record the resolution with its reason. Step 4: publish the merged base with the resolutions, so the merger can be audited. The demo runs a small worked example of the counting step with illustrative design values: it takes five experts with rules contributed of 60, 45, 70, 38, and 52, and reports the total, the mean, the smallest, and the largest. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"total_rules_design": 265, "mean_rules_design": 53.0, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the merger keeps modules, lists disagreements, and records resolutions, and the worked example shows the counting arithmetic. The five experts contribute 60, 45, 70, 38, and 52 rules, for a total of 265, a mean of 53.0, a smallest of 38, and a largest of 70. The result that matters is the design judgment: a merger that records its resolutions can be audited, and one that resolves silently cannot, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: largest: 70, mean: 53.0, n: 5, smallest: 38, total: 265.

When It Applies

The Signature Multi-Expert Merger design applies as a study for anyone combining several experts: it shows how to modularize, list, and resolve, and gives the arithmetic for checking the work. It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply where the disagreements cannot be resolved, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Limits

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The merger is a design, not a system, so it has never merged real experts, and its counts are illustrative. The design assumes the disagreements can be resolved by a stated rule, which is not always true, and it cannot handle experts whose knowledge is contradictory in a way no rule can settle. The design is a study of how a merger should be built, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such a merger exists or works.

Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of disagreement: experts disagree because they have different experience, and the disagreement is information, not noise. The design follows the public evidence that a rule base built from one expert is thinner than the expert it imitates, and it answers by combining several, with the disagreements made visible rather than hidden.     total = round(sum(counts), 4)

Signature Rule Auditor: A Design for Checking Rule Bases JAH-ESYSS-000021 · 21

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Rule Auditor is a Signature-made design for auditing a rule base, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design gives the auditor four checks: rules that never fire, rules that contradict each other, cases that no rule covers, and rules whose author and date are missing. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of validation and verification from the 1970s and 1980s, when the maintenance problems of XCON showed what happens when a rule base is not checked. The design takes the four checks the public record describes, and makes them a single audit with a report, so that the health of a rule base can be read at a glance. Working demo program

import hashlib
ledger = [('audit 1', 18), ('audit 2', 24), ('audit 3', 12), ('audit 4', 29), ('audit 5', 16)]
def compute():
    counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
    total = round(sum(counts), 4)
    mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
    smallest = min(counts)
    largest = max(counts)
    n = len(counts)
    out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class ProgJAHESYSS000021:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        ledger = [('audit 1', 18), ('audit 2', 24), ('audit 3', 12), ('audit 4', 29), ('audit 5', 16)]
        def compute():
            counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
            total = round(sum(counts), 4)
            mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
            smallest = min(counts)
            largest = max(counts)
            n = len(counts)
            out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(ProgJAHESYSS000021().run())

Signature Case-Based Reasoner: A Design for Reasoning from Cases JAH-ESYSS-000022 · 22

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Case-Based Reasoner is a Signature-made design for reasoning from past cases rather than from rules, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design keeps a library of solved cases, and when a new case arrives it finds the most similar past case, adapts its solution, and stores the result as a new case. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of case-based reasoning, which grew up alongside rule-based systems as an alternative for domains where the knowledge is in the cases rather than in the rules. The design takes the retrieve-reuse-revise-retain cycle the public record describes, and makes each step explicit, so that the reasoning can be checked. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Case-Based Reasoner is a Signature-made design for reasoning from past cases rather than from rules, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design keeps a library of solved cases, and when a new case arrives it finds the most similar past case, adapts its solution, and stores the result as a new case. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature Case-Based Reasoner, a Signature-made design-study for reasoning from past cases, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the new case and search the library for the most similar past case. Step 2: reuse the past case's solution, adapting it to the differences. Step 3: revise the adapted solution against the facts of the new case. Step 4: retain the solved case in the library, so the system learns. The demo runs a small worked example of the scoring step with illustrative design values: it takes five retrieved cases with similarity scores of 0.92, 0.78, 0.88, 0.65, and 0.81 against reference scores of 0.9, 0.8, 0.86, 0.67, and 0.8, and reports the mean absolute error and the root mean square error. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"mae_design": 0.018, "rmse_design": 0.0184, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the reasoner retrieves, reuses, revises, and retains, and the worked example shows the scoring arithmetic. The gaps are 0.02, 0.02, 0.02, 0.02, and 0.01, giving a mean absolute error of 0.018 and a root mean square error of 0.0184, with a largest gap of 0.02. The result that matters is the design judgment: a reasoner that retains what it learns grows more useful, and one that does not stays as thin as the day it was built, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: mae: 0.018, max_err: 0.02, n: 5, rmse: 0.0184.

When It Applies

The Signature Case-Based Reasoner design applies as a study for anyone building a system that reasons from precedent: it shows how to retrieve, reuse, revise, and retain. It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply where no similar case exists, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Limits

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The reasoner is a design, not a system, so it has never solved a real case, and its scores are illustrative. The design assumes the library contains a similar case, which is not always true, and the adaptation is a judgment that the design cannot make on its own. The design is a study of how a case-based reasoner should be built, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such a reasoner exists or works.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of error measurement. The five gaps are 0.02, 0.02, 0.02, 0.02, and 0.01, which sum to 0.09, so the mean absolute error is 0.09 divided by 5, which is 0.018. The squared gaps are 0.0004, 0.0004, 0.0004, 0.0004, and 0.0001, which sum to 0.0017, so the mean squared error is 0.00034 and the root mean square error is the square root of 0.00034, which is 0.0184. The largest single gap is 0.02. These are illustrative design values, and their transparency is the point: a design whose arithmetic can be checked is a design that can be argued with. Formal statement: MAE = (1/n) * sum |pred_i - actual_i|; RMSE = sqrt((1/n) * sum (pred_i - actual_i)^2); max error = max |pred_i - actual_i|. The equation states the forecast-error check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted and actual pairs, averages the absolute errors for the MAE, and takes the square root of the averaged squared errors for the RMSE.

Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of reasoning from precedent: a new problem is often like an old one, and the old solution, adapted, is a good starting point. The design follows the public evidence that case-based reasoning suits domains where the knowledge is in the cases, and it makes the adaptation explicit, so that the reuse can be checked. The design is a study, so its claims are design claims, and they are judged by whether the design is complete and coherent, not by whether a real system was measured. Scientific relation: A forecast is judged by the gap between what was predicted and what happened; the MAE averages that gap and the RMSE punishes large gaps more heavily.. Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(0.92, 0.9), (0.78, 0.8), (0.88, 0.86), (0.65, 0.67), (0.81, 0.8)]
def compute():
    errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
    mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
    rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
    max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
    n = len(pairs)
    out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Signature Knowledge Graph Shell: A Design for Linked Knowledge JAH-ESYSS-000023 · 23

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Knowledge Graph Shell is a Signature-made design for knowledge as a graph, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design takes the semantic-network idea, concepts linked by labeled relationships, and gives it a shell: the graph is stored, queried by following the links, and checked for consistency, so that the knowledge can be explored rather than merely listed. History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of semantic networks from 1968 through the modern knowledge graph. The design takes the graph's strength, that relationships are first-class, and addresses its weakness, that graphs grow tangled, by requiring every link to carry its type and its source. Description

The Signature Knowledge Graph Shell is a Signature-made design for knowledge as a graph, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The Problem

What is the Signature Knowledge Graph Shell, a Signature-made design-study for knowledge as a graph, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the domain's concepts and name them, with definitions a human can check. Step 2: link the concepts by typed relationships, stating what each link means and where it came from. Step 3: query the graph by following the links, and check the result for consistency. Step 4: report the answer with the path of links behind it, so the reasoning can be checked. The demo runs a small worked example of the counting step with illustrative design values: it takes five graph sections with links of 320, 480, 260, 540, and 390, and reports the total, the mean, the smallest, and the largest. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"total_links_design": 1990, "mean_links_design": 398.0, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the shell stores, links, queries, and checks, and the worked example shows the counting arithmetic. The five sections hold 320, 480, 260, 540, and 390 links, for a total of 1990, a mean of 398.0, a smallest of 260, and a largest of 540. The result that matters is the design judgment: a graph whose links are typed and sourced can be checked, and one whose links are not cannot, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: largest: 540, mean: 398.0, n: 5, smallest: 260, total: 1990.

When It Applies

The Signature Knowledge Graph Shell design applies as a study for anyone building a knowledge graph: it shows how to type, source, and query links, and gives the arithmetic for checking the work. It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply where the relationships cannot be typed, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Limits

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The shell is a design, not a system, so its graph has never answered a real query, and its counts are illustrative. The design assumes the relationships can be typed, which is not always true, and a large graph grows tangled faster than it can be checked. The design is a study of how a knowledge graph should be built, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such a shell exists or works.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of a ledger. The five sections hold 320, 480, 260, 540, and 390 links, which sum to 1990, and 1990 divided by 5 is 398.0. The smallest section holds 260 links and the largest holds 540. These are illustrative design values, and their transparency is the point: a design whose arithmetic can be checked is a design that can be argued with, and a design that cannot be checked is only a picture. Formal statement: total = sum of the counts; mean = total / n; smallest = min of the counts; largest = max of the counts. The equation states the ledger check the demo performs: it adds the named counts, divides by how many there are for the mean, and picks out the smallest and the largest.

Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of relationships as knowledge: a concept's meaning lies partly in its links to other concepts, and a graph makes those links explicit and traversable. The design follows the public evidence that semantic networks capture relationships well, and it adds the typing and sourcing, because an untyped link is a claim without a meaning. The design is a study, so its claims are design claims, and they are judged by whether the design is complete and coherent, not by whether a real system was measured. Working demo program

import hashlib
ledger = [('section 1', 320), ('section 2', 480), ('section 3', 260), ('section 4', 540), ('section 5', 390)]
def compute():
    counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
    total = round(sum(counts), 4)
    mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
    smallest = min(counts)
    largest = max(counts)
    n = len(counts)
    out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class ProgJAHESYSS000023:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        ledger = [('section 1', 320), ('section 2', 480), ('section 3', 260), ('section 4', 540), ('section 5', 390)]
        def compute():
            counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
            total = round(sum(counts), 4)
            mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
            smallest = min(counts)
            largest = max(counts)
            n = len(counts)
            out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(ProgJAHESYSS000023().run())

Signature Expert System Workbench: A Design for the Whole Craft JAH-ESYSS-000024 · 24

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Expert System Workbench is a Signature-made design for the whole craft of building expert systems, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design gathers the parts the other designs in this edition describe separately, the shell, the elicitation studio, the explanation facility, the validation harness, and the auditor, into one workbench, so that a builder can move from elicitation to testing without changing tools. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of expert systems from DENDRAL in 1965 through the business-rules engines of the 2000s. The design takes the lesson of that history, that the craft is the whole cycle from elicitation to maintenance, and makes the cycle the architecture: one workbench, five stations, and a ledger that follows every rule from the interview where it was born to the audit where it is checked. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Expert System Workbench is a Signature-made design for the whole craft of building expert systems, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design gathers the parts the other designs in this edition describe separately, the shell, the elicitation studio, the explanation facility, the validation harness, and the auditor, into one workbench, so that a builder can move from elicitation to testing without changing tools. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature Expert System Workbench, a Signature-made design-study for the whole craft of building expert systems, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: elicit the knowledge through cases, in the studio. Step 2: write the rules in the shell, with the engine ready to run them. Step 3: explain the conclusions through the facility, so they can be checked. Step 4: validate the rule base through the harness, and audit it through the auditor, on a schedule. The demo runs a small worked example of the counting step with illustrative design values: it takes five workbench stations with rules handled of 120, 95, 140, 110, and 130, and reports the total, the mean, the smallest, and the largest. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"total_rules_design": 595, "mean_rules_design": 119.0, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The five stations handle 120, 95, 140, 110, and 130 rules, for a total of 595, a mean of 119.0, a smallest of 95, and a largest of 140. The result that matters is the design judgment: a craft needs one workbench for the whole cycle, because a cycle split across tools loses the ledger that ties a rule to its interview and its audit, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: largest: 140, mean: 119.0, n: 5, smallest: 95, total: 595.

import hashlib
ledger = [('elicitation', 120), ('writing', 95), ('explanation', 140), ('validation', 110), ('audit', 130)]
def compute():
    counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
    total = round(sum(counts), 4)
    mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
    smallest = min(counts)
    largest = max(counts)
    n = len(counts)
    out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class ProgJAHESYSS000024:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        ledger = [('elicitation', 120), ('writing', 95), ('explanation', 140), ('validation', 110), ('audit', 130)]
        def compute():
            counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
            total = round(sum(counts), 4)
            mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
            smallest = min(counts)
            largest = max(counts)
            n = len(counts)
            out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(ProgJAHESYSS000024().run())
